```json
      "content": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\subsection{Experimental Setup}\n\n\\paragraph{Model and Predictor.}\nWe evaluate on LLaMA-3.1-8B~\\cite{grattafiori2024llama3}, which uses SwiGLU FFN blocks~\\cite{shazeer2020glu} with $d_{\\text{model}}=4096$ and $d_{\\text{ff}}=14336$ across 32 layers.\nThe sparsity predictor is a two-layer MLP with 2.37M parameters per layer (75.9M total), implemented as Linear($4096 \\to 128$) + GELU + Linear($128 \\to 14336$), trained to produce per-neuron importance scores that minimize output-distribution divergence under sparsity.\n\n\\paragraph{Training.}\nAll predictors are trained for 1000 steps on WikiText-103 with sequence length 2048 and batch size 4, using AdamW~\\cite{loshchilov2019adamw} with learning rate $1 \\times 10^{-3}$, weight decay $5 \\times 10^{-2}$, and cosine decay.\nThe KL predictor uses Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation~\\cite{jang2017gumbel} with temperature $\\tau=1.0$ annealed to $0.1$ over training.\n\n\\paragraph{Evaluation.}\nWe report perplexity on WikiText-2~\\cite{merity2017pointer} (seq\\_len=2048) and downstream accuracy on ARC-Challenge (25-shot), WinoGrande (5-shot), MMLU (5-shot), and GSM8K (8-shot).\n\n\\paragraph{Inference Protocol.}\nAt inference, the predictor produces per-neuron scores that are passed to TEAL's global top-$k$ allocation mechanism~\\cite{liu2025teal}, which distributes the sparsity budget across layers non-uniformly.\nWe term this \\textit{predictor-TEAL}: the predictor replaces TEAL's magnitude-based scoring while retaining its allocation strategy.\n\n\\paragraph{Baselines.}\nWe compare against: (1) \\textbf{Vanilla TEAL}~\\cite{liu2025teal}, which uses activation magnitudes directly; (2) \\textbf{BCE predictor} (DejaVu-style~\\cite{liu2023dejavu}), trained with per-neuron binary cross-entropy on magnitude-based oracle masks for 1000 and 2700 steps; (3) \\textbf{Uniform allocation}, which distributes the sparsity budget equally across layers; and (4) \\textbf{WINA}~\\cite{liu2025teal}, an importance-weighted variant.\n\n\n\\subsection{Main Results}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:main} presents the primary comparison. We recommend uniform allocation for deployment (best downstream preservation); global allocation is analyzed to understand allocation-quality tradeoffs (\\S\\ref{sec:scaling}).\nAt 30\\% sparsity, KL distillation with global TEAL allocation achieves PPL 8.96$\\pm$0.02 (3-seed) and reduces TEAL's degradation from dense (10.97 $\\to$ 8.96, an 18\\% improvement). % exp_id: kl30_3seed_global\nThe advantage holds across additional downstream benchmarks (Appendix~\\ref{app:downstream}).\nUniform allocation at 30\\% provides stable downstream performance (MMLU 40.7$\\pm$1.3\\%, 3-seed) while global top-$k$ exhibits high seed variance (35.7$\\pm$9.5\\%), with late-layer starvation (L31 retention 28.4\\%) interacting with predictor initialization (\\S\\ref{sec:scaling}). % exp_id: bench_kl30_uniform, kl30_3seed\n\nAt 50\\% sparsity, KL distillation over vanilla TEAL widens the PPL advantage: 11.79 vs.\\ 23.31, a 49\\% reduction. % exp_id: bench_kl50_global, bench_teal50\nThe predictor generalizes to C4 validation at both 30\\% (PPL 16.03$\\pm$0.01, 2-seed) and 50\\% (52.5\\% over TEAL; Appendix~\\ref{app:ood}).\nHowever, all methods exhibit downstream collapse on MMLU (28.1$\\pm$0.2\\% vs.\\ 28.0$\\pm$0.6\\%, both $\\approx$ random, 3-seed) and GSM8K ($<$2.2\\%); this is a capacity-limited regime where no allocation strategy can recover. % exp_id: bench_kl50_global, d030_kl50_uniform, multi_seed_kl50_global\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Main results on LLaMA-3.1-8B. PPL$\\downarrow$ on WikiText-2; accuracy$\\uparrow$ on downstream tasks. \\textbf{Bold}: best among compensation-free methods per sparsity level. BCE follows the DejaVu~\\cite{liu2023dejavu} predictor pipeline (per-neuron BCE on magnitude oracles) with a FastForward-style~\\cite{gautam2026fastforward} compensation network (67.2M params) applied post-masking; no TEAL allocation. Entries with $\\pm$ report 3-seed mean$\\pm$std ($s \\in \\{42, 123, 456\\}$); 50\\% uniform PPL is 3-seed ($11.92 \\pm 0.07$; Appendix~\\ref{app:stability}); remaining entries use seed 42.}\n\\label{tab:main}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{\n\\begin{tabular}{llccccc}\n\\toprule\nSparsity & Method & PPL$\\downarrow$ & ARC-c & WG & MMLU & GSM8K \\\\\n\\midrule\n0\\% & Dense & 6.12 & 58.28 & 78.06 & 65.27 & 48.75 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{30\\%}\n& TEAL vanilla & 10.97 & 42.32 & 66.06 & 38.61 & 7.88 \\\\ % exp_id: bench_teal30\n& KL global (Ours) & 8.96$\\pm$0.02 & \\textbf{43.8$\\pm$3.6} & \\textbf{70.2$\\pm$0.9} & 35.7$\\pm$9.5 & \\textbf{8.0$\\pm$0.7} \\\\ % exp_id: kl30_3seed_global\n& KL uniform & \\textbf{8.82$\\pm$0.02} & 43.7$\\pm$2.5 & 68.9$\\pm$0.4 & \\textbf{40.7$\\pm$1.3} & 7.5$\\pm$0.1 \\\\ % exp_id: bench_kl30_uniform, kl30_s123_uniform, kl30_s456_uniform\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{50\\%}\n& TEAL vanilla & 23.31 & 28.41 & 53.04 & \\textbf{28.32} & 1.97 \\\\ % exp_id: bench_teal50\n& KL global (Ours) & \\textbf{11.79} & \\textbf{32.85} & \\textbf{63.46} & 28.1$\\pm$0.2 & 1.29 \\\\ % exp_id: multi_seed_kl50_global\n& KL uniform & 11.96 & 32.76 & 62.90 & 28.0$\\pm$0.6 & \\textbf{2.12} \\\\ % exp_id: multi_seed_kl50_uniform\n& BCE (2700 steps)$^\\ast$ & 28.13$\\pm$0.20 & 27.6$\\pm$0.6 & 51.8$\\pm$0.6 & 26.1$\\pm$0.5 & 2.1$\\pm$0.2 \\\\ % exp_id: bce50_3seed\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\subsection{Extended Training Analysis}\n\\label{sec:isocompute}\n\nTo test whether BCE simply requires more compute, we train it for 2700 steps (2.71$\\times$ the KL step budget, approximately equal wall-clock time: $\\sim$46 min BCE vs.\\ $\\sim$45 min KL on RTX PRO 6000).\nThe BCE predictor achieves PPL 28.13$\\pm$0.20 (3-seed), still 2.39$\\times$ worse than KL at 1000 steps (PPL 11.79). % exp_id: bce50_3seed\nThe difference is systematic, not due to architectural or compute limitations: both predictors use identical architectures and TEAL allocation, yet BCE achieves +7pp oracle F1 (0.613 vs.\\ 0.540) with 139\\% worse PPL (28.13 vs.\\ 11.79). KL's near-random precision (0.51) is consistent with learning a mask optimized for distributional fidelity rather than magnitude matching. % exp_id: f1_oracle_analysis, bce_isocompute_s42\nCoupled training correlates with better output preservation, but disentangling gradient coupling from oracle quality remains open (BCE uses magnitude oracles while KL optimizes output directly; an ablation using KL-derived masks as BCE targets would isolate the effect).\n\n\n\\subsection{Ablation Studies}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:ablation} isolates the contribution of each design choice at 50\\% sparsity.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Ablation at 50\\% sparsity on LLaMA-3.1-8B (WikiText-2 PPL$\\downarrow$, unified benchmark protocol).}\n\\label{tab:ablation}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\nConfiguration & PPL$\\downarrow$ & $\\Delta$ from Ours \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{l}{\\textit{KL-based variants}} \\\\\nForward KL + Gumbel (Ours) & \\textbf{11.79} & --- \\\\ % exp_id: bench_kl50_global\nJSD (symmetric) + Gumbel & 12.54 & +0.75 \\\\ % exp_id: jsd_ablation_s42\nReverse KL + Gumbel & 12.80 & +1.01 \\\\ % exp_id: reverse_kl_ablation_s42\nForward KL + STE & 15.77 & +3.98 \\\\ % exp_id: ste_baseline_s42\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{l}{\\textit{BCE-based variants}} \\\\\nBCE + Compensation (hard mask) & 25.44 & +13.65 \\\\ % exp_id: bce_comp_staged_s42\nBCE (no compensation) & 27.97 & +16.18 \\\\ % exp_id: eval_bce_v3_s42\nBCE + Gumbel + Penalty (no KL) & 38.02 & +26.23 \\\\ % exp_id: bce_gumbel_lagrangian_isolation\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Gumbel vs.\\ STE.}\nReplacing Gumbel-Sigmoid with STE~\\cite{bengio2013ste} degrades PPL from 11.79 to 15.77 (+3.98), accounting for 34.5\\% of the improvement over vanilla TEAL. % exp_id: ste_baseline_s42\n\n\\paragraph{Divergence Direction.}\nForward KL (mode-covering) outperforms JSD (+6.4\\%) and reverse KL (+8.6\\%), consistent with the SNR analysis in \\S\\ref{sec:method:gradient}: complete teacher supervision is associated with better global allocation quality. % exp_id: jsd_ablation_s42, reverse_kl_ablation_s42\nThis contrasts with MiniLLM~\\cite{gu2024minillm} (reverse KL for generation); in mask prediction, mode-covering ensures no important neuron is omitted.\n\n\\paragraph{SPON Contribution.}\nAdding SPON~\\cite{xu2026spon} bias correction reduces PPL from 12.08 to 11.79 (2.4\\% relative); see \\S\\ref{sec:spon} for decomposition.\\footnote{SPON analysis uses \\texttt{evaluate.py}; main results use the unified benchmark.} % exp_id: spon_2048_s42, mvp_kl_norm_v2_s42\n\n\\paragraph{Loss Function Isolation.}\nBCE with identical Gumbel-Sigmoid and penalty yields PPL 38.02, worse than vanilla BCE (27.97): the penalty interferes with per-neuron optimization without global coordination. % exp_id: bce_gumbel_lagrangian_isolation\nThe loss effect ($\\Delta$PPL=26.23) is $6.6\\times$ the relaxation effect ($\\Delta$PPL=3.98).\n\n\\paragraph{Compensation Redundancy.}\nAdding compensation~\\cite{gautam2026fastforward} (+67.2M params) to KL provides no benefit at either tested sparsity: at 50\\%, all five metrics are identical to KL-only; at 30\\%, PPL 8.97$\\pm$0.01 vs.\\ 8.96$\\pm$0.03 (2-seed). BCE requires compensation (27.97$\\to$25.44), yet remains $2.16\\times$ worse than KL without it. Training converges by step 700 (Appendix~\\ref{app:comp_convergence}). % exp_id: kl_comp_30_s42, kl_comp_30_s456, kl_comp_50_s42, bce_comp_staged_s42\n\n\\paragraph{Hyperparameter Robustness.}\nAppendix~\\ref{app:db_sensitivity} confirms robustness to predictor capacity ($d_b$, $<$3\\%), temperature ($<$1.8\\%), and training steps (saturated by 800). % exp_id: db_sensitivity_sweep, tau_schedule_sweep, training_steps_sweep\n\n\n\\subsection{Sparsity Scaling and Regime Analysis}\n\\label{sec:scaling}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:scaling} and Figure~\\ref{fig:sparsity_sweep} show PPL as a function of sparsity for the KL predictor under global allocation and vanilla TEAL.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig2_sparsity_sweep.pdf}\n\\caption{Perplexity scaling with sparsity. The KL predictor's advantage grows disproportionately as more neurons are pruned.}\n\\label{fig:sparsity_sweep}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{PPL scaling with sparsity (WikiText-2). KL advantage grows disproportionately with sparsity.}\n\\label{tab:scaling}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\nSparsity & KL (Ours) & TEAL vanilla & Relative advantage \\\\\n\\midrule\n30\\% & \\textbf{8.96} & 10.97 & 18\\% \\\\ % exp_id: kl30_3seed_global\n50\\% & \\textbf{11.79} & 23.31 & 49\\% \\\\ % exp_id: bench_kl50_global\n70\\% & \\textbf{17.91} & 100.11 & 82\\% \\\\ % exp_id: kl_70_global_teal\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe KL advantage grows from 18\\% at 30\\% to 82\\% at 70\\%, where TEAL collapses (PPL 100.11) while the learned predictor maintains coherent allocation.\nIn absolute terms, KL/Dense degradation is $1.47\\times$ at 30\\%, $1.93\\times$ at 50\\%, and $2.93\\times$ at 70\\%, compared to TEAL/Dense ratios of $1.79\\times$, $3.81\\times$, and $16.4\\times$ respectively.\n\n\\paragraph{Three Operating Regions.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:phase} presents global vs.\\ uniform allocation at three sparsity levels and reveals qualitative shifts in allocation preference. Denser sampling (e.g., 35\\% sparsity) would be needed to precisely locate region boundaries.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Allocation analysis: global (TEAL) vs.\\ uniform per-layer allocation across sparsity levels. Entries with $\\pm$ report 3-seed mean$\\pm$std; remaining entries use seed 42. Each row uses a predictor trained at the respective sparsity target.}\n\\label{tab:phase}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{\n\\begin{tabular}{llccccc}\n\\toprule\nSparsity & Allocation & PPL$\\downarrow$ & ARC-c & WG & MMLU & GSM8K \\\\\n\\midrule\n0\\% & Dense & 6.12 & 58.28 & 78.06 & 65.27 & 48.75 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{30\\%}\n& TEAL vanilla & 10.97 & 42.32 & 66.06 & 38.61 & 7.88 \\\\ % exp_id: bench_teal30\n& KL global & 8.96$\\pm$0.02 & \\textbf{43.8$\\pm$3.6} & \\textbf{70.2$\\pm$0.9} & 35.7$\\pm$9.5 & \\textbf{8.0$\\pm$0.7} \\\\ % exp_id: kl30_3seed_global\n& KL uniform & \\textbf{8.82$\\pm$0.02} & 43.7$\\pm$2.5 & 68.9$\\pm$0.4 & \\textbf{40.7$\\pm$1.3} & 7.5$\\pm$0.1 \\\\ % exp_id: bench_kl30_uniform, kl30_s123_uniform, kl30_s456_uniform\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{2}{*}{40\\%}\n& KL global & 10.12 & 40.96 & 65.35 & \\textbf{38.06} & \\textbf{5.00} \\\\ % exp_id: bench_kl40_global\n& KL uniform & \\textbf{8.36} & \\textbf{41.81} & \\textbf{67.17} & 30.68 & 3.94 \\\\ % exp_id: d030_kl40_uniform\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{50\\%}\n& TEAL vanilla & 23.31 & 28.41 & 53.04 & \\textbf{28.32} & 1.97 \\\\ % exp_id: bench_teal50\n& KL global & \\textbf{11.79} & \\textbf{32.85} & \\textbf{63.46} & 28.1$\\pm$0.2 & 1.29 \\\\ % exp_id: multi_seed_kl50_global\n& KL uniform & 11.96 & 32.76 & 62.90 & 28.0$\\pm$0.6 & \\textbf{2.12} \\\\ % exp_id: multi_seed_kl50_uniform\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe allocation preference is \\emph{non-monotonic}. We observe three qualitative behaviors at the sampled sparsity levels (7--8B scale):\n\n\\noindent\\textbf{Budget-surplus ($\\leq$30\\%).}\nUniform allocation provides $7.5\\times$ smaller cross-seed MMLU $\\sigma$ (1.3pp vs.\\ 9.5pp), eliminating catastrophic seed-dependent degradation under global top-$k$ (MMLU 35.7$\\pm$9.5\\% vs.\\ 40.7$\\pm$1.3\\%, 3-seed); the instability is allocation-induced, not from the distillation objective (PPL cross-seed CV $<$ 0.3\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:lagrangian}). % exp_id: kl30_3seed\n\n\\noindent\\textbf{Budget-constrained ($\\sim$40\\%).}\nThe preference reverses: global achieves MMLU 38.06\\% vs.\\ uniform's 30.68\\% (+7.4pp). % exp_id: bench_kl40_global, d030_kl40_uniform\nPPL remains low (8.36) but downstream accuracy degrades; PPL is a poor proxy for reasoning.\n\n\\noindent\\textbf{Capacity-limited ($\\geq$50\\%).}\nGlobal/uniform gap vanishes (MMLU 28.1$\\pm$0.2\\% vs.\\ 28.0$\\pm$0.6\\%, $\\sigma\\leq$0.6pp; contrast 9.5pp at 30\\%), consistent with capacity limitation. % exp_id: multi_seed_kl50_global, multi_seed_kl50_uniform\nLate-layer starvation persists across levels (Figure~\\ref{fig:retention}; Appendix~\\ref{app:db_sensitivity}).\n\n\n\\subsection{SPON Orthogonality Analysis}\n\\label{sec:spon}\n\nAt 50\\% sparsity, the predictor's hidden-state perturbation (L2=482.65) and SPON's additive correction (L2=76.2, cosine similarity 0.196) are weakly correlated ($R^2$=0.038); SPON yields only 2.4\\% PPL improvement (12.08$\\to$11.79); the KL predictor already captures most recoverable signal. % exp_id: spon_decomposition, spon_2048_s42, mvp_kl_norm_v2_s42\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-Architecture Validation.}\n\\label{sec:cross_arch}\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross_arch} evaluates on Mistral-7B-v0.3~\\cite{jiang2023mistral} (GQA + sliding window, dense PPL 5.52).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Cross-architecture comparison (WikiText-2 PPL$\\downarrow$). Adv = TEAL\\,/\\,KL ratio. LLaMA uses global top-$k$; Mistral uses uniform allocation (matched within each architecture).}\n\\label{tab:cross_arch}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{l@{\\hspace{4pt}}cc@{\\hspace{3pt}}c@{\\hspace{6pt}}|@{\\hspace{6pt}}cc@{\\hspace{3pt}}c}\n\\toprule\n & \\multicolumn{3}{c}{Mistral-7B} & \\multicolumn{3}{c}{LLaMA-8B} \\\\\n & KL & TEAL & Adv & KL & TEAL & Adv \\\\\n\\midrule\n30\\% & \\textbf{6.77} & 8.38 & 1.24$\\times$ & \\textbf{8.96} & 10.97 & 1.22$\\times$ \\\\ % exp_id: kl_30_mistral, teal_30_mistral, kl30_3seed_global\n50\\% & \\textbf{8.65} & 18.31 & 2.12$\\times$ & \\textbf{11.79} & 23.31 & 1.98$\\times$ \\\\ % exp_id: kl_50_mistral, teal_50_mistral\n70\\% & \\textbf{13.33} & 61.72 & 4.63$\\times$ & \\textbf{17.91} & 100.11 & 5.59$\\times$ \\\\ % exp_id: kl_70_mistral, teal_70_mistral\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe KL advantage ratio is consistent across architectures (within 8\\% at $\\leq$50\\% sparsity).\n\n\\paragraph{Scale Generalization: Qwen-2.5-14B.}\n\\label{sec:14b}\nTable~\\ref{tab:14b} evaluates Qwen-2.5-14B~\\cite{qwen2024qwen25} at 30\\%: KL's PPL advantage generalizes ($1.47\\times$ dense), but MMLU falls below TEAL/BCE (63.7 vs.\\ 71.8\\%), and uniform recovers only +0.8pp. The gap appears intrinsic to KL's output-preservation objective rather than allocation-induced.\nHigher knowledge density at 14B may cause KL's output-preservation objective to sacrifice task-specific pathways that 8B retains at 30\\%; whether this generalizes requires further study.\nBCE collapses to 6.4\\% native sparsity (\\S\\ref{sec:method:gradient}); Table~\\ref{tab:14b} uses forced global top-$k$. % exp_id: qwen14b_kl30_s42, qwen14b_bce30_s42, qwen14b_bce30_s42_v2, qwen14b_teal30_eval, qwen14b_kl30_uniform, qwen14b_teal30_uniform\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Qwen-2.5-14B at 30\\% sparsity. \\textbf{Bold}: best sparse per column per group.}\n\\label{tab:14b}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\nMethod & PPL$\\downarrow$ & ARC-c & WG & MMLU & GSM8K \\\\\n\\midrule\nDense & 5.19 & 67.58 & 81.06 & 79.77 & 87.57 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\emph{Global top-$k$ allocation}} \\\\\nKL (Ours) & \\textbf{7.62} & 57.17 & 72.06 & 63.72 & 48.82 \\\\ % exp_id: qwen14b_kl30_s42\nTEAL & 8.73 & 56.83 & \\textbf{72.69} & \\textbf{71.83} & 49.51 \\\\ % exp_id: qwen14b_teal30_eval\nBCE & 8.76 & \\textbf{57.59} & 72.61 & 71.67 & \\textbf{53.07} \\\\ % exp_id: qwen14b_bce30_s42_eval\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\emph{Uniform allocation}} \\\\\nKL (Ours) & \\textbf{7.63} & 54.44 & \\textbf{72.45} & 64.49 & --- \\\\ % exp_id: qwen14b_kl30_uniform\nTEAL & 8.01 & \\textbf{55.29} & 69.85 & \\textbf{66.34} & --- \\\\ % exp_id: qwen14b_teal30_uniform\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\enlargethispage{10\\baselineskip}\n\\paragraph{Inference Efficiency.}\nThe pipeline adds 6.4\\% overhead while removing the compensation network ($-$2.7\\% latency, $-$67.2M params; 1	\section{Conclusion}
```
```text
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	\vspace{-4pt}
4	
5	KL distillation replaces per-neuron BCE with output-distribution coupling: a predictor ($<$1\% of base) halves TEAL's perplexity across 7--14B, with compensation (+67.2M params) redundant at both tested sparsity levels.
6	At 30\%, uniform allocation delivers $7.5\times$ smaller cross-seed $\sigma$; allocation-aware evaluation is essential since perplexity alone is a poor downstream proxy.
7	At 14B, the PPL advantage persists but an MMLU gap emerges, a scale-dependent tradeoff warranting further investigation.
8	
9	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01XVFa8baquduAbJEqx1w8m7`</summary>

```text
1	\section*{Limitations}
2	
3	Our primary evaluation uses WikiText-2 perplexity, and downstream benchmarks reveal that perplexity is a poor proxy for reasoning ability at moderate sparsity: 40\% sparsity achieves PPL 8.36 but MMLU already degrades, motivating 30\% as the recommended operating point for downstream fidelity.
4	Training requires 1000 steps on WikiText-103 ($\sim$45 minutes on RTX PRO 6000 for KL, $\sim$17 minutes for BCE), but a separate predictor must be trained for each sparsity target.
5	Our recommended operating point (30\% sparsity, uniform allocation) is validated at 7--8B scale; at 14B, the MMLU gap persists regardless of allocation strategy (\S\ref{sec:14b}). Scaling to 70B remains untested.
6	Out-of-distribution evaluation (Appendix~\ref{app:ood}) covers 30\% and 50\% sparsity on C4; extending to 70\% is left to future work.
7	Our attribution of KL's advantage to gradient coupling (\S\ref{sec:method:gradient}) is supported by SNR measurements and the BCE isolation ablation, but does not fully disentangle coupling from oracle quality: BCE uses magnitude-based oracles while KL directly optimizes output preservation, and an ablation using KL-derived masks as BCE targets would more precisely isolate the coupling effect.
8	At 30\% sparsity, global allocation exhibits 9.5pp cross-seed MMLU standard deviation (3-seed); allocation strategy interacts with predictor initialization in task-specific ways. This variance is MMLU-specific, as ARC, WinoGrande, and GSM8K show stable performance across seeds under both allocation strategies.
9	While we motivate our work with inference speedup potential, actual wall-clock acceleration depends on sparse kernel implementations (e.g., Triton, cuSPARSE) and hardware-specific memory access patterns; this work focuses on prediction quality rather than systems optimization.
10	Future directions include N:M structured sparsity for hardware-native acceleration, scaling to 13B+ models, and training a single predictor that serves multiple sparsity targets simultaneously.
11	
```

</details>

```text
1	\section*{Ethics Statement}
2	
3	This work proposes a training method for sparsity predictors in pretrained language models, evaluated on publicly available models (LLaMA-3.1-8B, Mistral-7B-v0.3) and standard benchmarks (WikiText-2, WikiText-103, C4, ARC, WinoGrande, MMLU, GSM8K).
4	No new data collection, annotation, or human subjects are involved.
5	The method does not alter model outputs beyond the sparsity-induced degradation we analyze; it introduces no new generation capabilities or safety risks beyond those inherent in the base models.
6	Compute requirements are modest (1000 training steps, $\sim$45 minutes on a single GPU for KL training), presenting minimal environmental concern relative to the pretrained models we evaluate on.
7	We release no model weights or user-facing systems; our contribution is analytical (understanding KL distillation for sparsity) rather than deployment-oriented.
8	
9	\section*{Responsible NLP Research Checklist}
10	
11	\paragraph{A. Limitations and Risks.}
12	\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
13	\item[\textbf{A1.}] Limitations are discussed in the Limitations section. Key limitations: evaluation limited to 7--8B models; perplexity is an imperfect proxy for downstream quality; wall-clock speedup requires sparse kernel support not implemented here.
14	\item[\textbf{A2.}] No foreseeable risks specific to this work beyond those inherent in the base language models. The method does not enable new capabilities or applications.
15	\end{itemize}
16	
17	\paragraph{B. Scientific Artifacts.}
18	\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
19	\item[\textbf{B1.}] Yes. All models (LLaMA-3.1-8B~\cite{grattafiori2024llama3}, Mistral-7B-v0.3~\cite{jiang2023mistral}), datasets (WikiText~\cite{merity2017pointer}, C4~\cite{raffel2020c4}), and methods (TEAL~\cite{liu2025teal}, SPON~\cite{xu2026spon}) are cited with original references.
20	\item[\textbf{B2.}] LLaMA-3.1 is released under the Llama 3.1 Community License; Mistral-7B under Apache 2.0. WikiText and C4 are publicly available research datasets. Our use is consistent with their intended purposes.
21	\item[\textbf{B3.}] N/A. We do not release new artifacts in this submission.
22	\item[\textbf{B4.}] N/A. No personally identifiable information or offensive content is involved.
23	\item[\textbf{B5.}] N/A. No new datasets created.
24	\item[\textbf{B6.}] N/A. No crowdsourced data.
25	\end{itemize}
26	
27	\paragraph{C. Computational Experiments.}
28	\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
29	\item[\textbf{C1.}] Yes. Model sizes (8B base, 75.9M predictor), training compute (1000 steps, batch 4, seq 2048), optimizer (AdamW~\cite{loshchilov2019adamw}, lr=$10^{-3}$, cosine decay), and GPU (NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell for both training and latency measurement) are reported in \S4.1 and Appendix A.
30	\item[\textbf{C2.}] Yes. Hyperparameters are fully specified: bottleneck $d_b{=}128$, temperature schedule $\tau: 1.0 \to 0.1$, sparsity penalty parameters ($\epsilon{=}0.02$, $\lambda_{\max}$), weight decay $5{\times}10^{-2}$.
31	\item[\textbf{C3.}] Yes. Three-seed evaluation reported (PPL std=0.07, CV$<$1\%; Appendix E).
32	\item[\textbf{C4.}] Yes. All evaluation uses established metrics (perplexity, accuracy) on standard benchmarks with standard few-shot protocols (ARC 25-shot, WinoGrande 5-shot, MMLU 5-shot, GSM8K 8-shot).
33	\end{itemize}
34	
35	\paragraph{D. Human Subjects.}
36	\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
37	\item[\textbf{D1--D5.}] N/A. No human subjects, crowdsourcing, or annotation involved.
38	\end{itemize}
39	
40	\paragraph{E. AI Assistants.}
41	\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
42	\item[\textbf{E1.}] AI writing assistants were used for drafting and editing. All scientific claims, experimental results, and analyses were produced and verified by the authors.
43	\end{itemize}
44	
```

```text
I've successfully performed the web search. Here's a summary of the recent research on activation sparsity prediction for LLMs with KL distillation and efficient inference:

## Key Recent Papers (2024-2026)

### SparseInfer (November 2024)
SparseInfer is a simple, light-weight, and training-free predictor for activation sparsity of ReLU-fied LLMs, in which activation sparsity is predicted by comparing only the sign bits of inputs and weights. The proposed method achieves approximately faster inference speed over the state of the art, with negligible accuracy loss of within 1%p.

### R-Sparse (April 2025)
R-Sparse is a training-free activation sparsity approach capable of achieving high sparsity levels in advanced LLMs. Experiments on Llama-2/3 and Mistral models across ten diverse tasks demonstrate that R-Sparse achieves comparable performance at 50% model-level sparsity, resulting in a significant 43% end-to-end efficient improvements with customized kernels.

### Activation Pattern Clustering (July 2025)
A clustering-based activation pattern compression framework enables efficient prediction and utilization of activation sparsity by grouping similar activation patterns into a small set of representative clusters. The method achieves up to 79.34% clustering precision, outperforming standard binary clustering approaches while maintaining minimal degradation in perplexity (PPL) scores.

## KL Distillation Context
Knowledge distillation involves evaluating importance of neurons, trimming the least important ones, and distilling knowledge from the original LLM to the pruned model, where the student model mimics the output and/or the intermediate states of the teacher model. L2-based distillation approaches enable accurate recovery even at higher sparsities, across all model types.

These approaches collectively address the challenge of efficient LLM inference by leveraging activation sparsity in modern architectures.


REMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks.
```

```json
            "url": "https://proceedings.neurips.cc/paper_files/paper/2024/file/4822991365c962105b1b95b1107d30e5-Paper-Conference.pdf"

      "I've successfully performed the web search. Here's a summary of the recent research on activation sparsity prediction for LLMs with KL distillation and efficient inference:\n\n## Key Recent Papers (2024-2026)\n\n### SparseInfer (November 2024)\nSparseInfer is a simple, light-weight, and training-free predictor for activation sparsity of ReLU-fied LLMs, in which activation sparsity is predicted by comparing only the sign bits of inputs and weights. The proposed method achieves approximately faster inference speed over the state of the art, with negligible accuracy loss of within 1%p.\n\n### R-Sparse (April 2025)\nR-Sparse is a training-free activation sparsity approach capable of achieving high sparsity levels in advanced LLMs. Experiments on Llama-2/3 and Mistral models across ten diverse tasks demonstrate that R-Sparse achieves comparable performance at 50% model-level sparsity, resulting in a significant 43% end-to-end efficient improvements with customized kernels.\n\n### Activation Pattern Clustering (July 2025)\nA clustering-based activation pattern compression framework enables efficient prediction and utilization of activation sparsity by grouping similar activation patterns into a small set of representative clusters. The method achieves up to 79.34% clustering precision, outperforming standard binary clustering approaches while maintaining minimal degradation in perplexity (PPL) scores.\n\n## KL Distillation Context\nKnowledge distillation involves evaluating importance of neurons, trimming the least important ones, and distilling knowledge from the original LLM to the pruned model, where the student model mimics the output and/or the intermediate states of the teacher model. L2-based distillation approaches enable accurate recovery even at higher sparsities, across all model types.\n\nThese approaches collectively address the challenge of efficient LLM inference by leveraging activation sparsity in modern architectures."
```

```text
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/related_work.tex:5:CATS~\cite{lee2024cats} adapts per-layer thresholds for SiLU/SwiGLU activations; R-Sparse~\cite{zhang2025rsparse} predicts sparsity from rank-aware SVD projections; DIP~\cite{federici2025dip} combines dynamic input pruning with cache-aware masking; SparseInfer~\cite{shin2025sparseinfer} predicts activation sparsity patterns from attention outputs without additional training.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/related_work.tex:13:PowerInfer~\cite{song2024powerinfer} exploits power-law activation frequencies for GPU--CPU scheduling.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/related_work.tex:15:Both share the principle of frozen weights + learnable masks + KL training, but solve distinct optimization problems: MaskLLM finds optimal static N:M patterns within fixed groups, while we predict dynamic per-token masks under a global budget constraint.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/related_work.tex:16:The structural mismatch we characterize (per-neuron gradient decomposition vs.\ global top-$k$) does not arise in MaskLLM's per-group categorical setting, where N:M patterns eliminate cross-group budget competition.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/related_work.tex:25:\paragraph{Activation Function Engineering.}
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/related_work.tex:30:\paragraph{Weight Pruning, Distillation, and Routing.}
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/related_work.tex:31:Wanda~\cite{sun2024wanda} and SparseGPT~\cite{frantar2023sparsegpt} produce static weight masks; Switch Transformers~\cite{fedus2022switch} and Mixture-of-Depths~\cite{raposo2024mod} achieve conditional computation through learned routing.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/related_work.tex:32:KL-based distillation~\cite{kim2016sequencekd,sanh2019distilbert,gu2024minillm,agarwal2024gkd} trains compact students; SparseLoRA~\cite{khaki2025sparselora} exploits contextual sparsity for fine-tuning.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/experiments.tex:10:\paragraph{Training.}
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/experiments.tex:12:The KL predictor uses Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation~\cite{jang2017gumbel} with temperature $\tau=1.0$ annealed to $0.1$ over training.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/experiments.tex:67:The difference is systematic, not due to architectural or compute limitations: both predictors use identical architectures and TEAL allocation, yet BCE achieves +7pp oracle F1 (0.613 vs.\ 0.540) with 139\% worse PPL (28.13 vs.\ 11.79). KL's near-random precision (0.51) is consistent with learning a mask optimized for distributional fidelity rather than magnitude matching. % exp_id: f1_oracle_analysis, bce_isocompute_s42
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/experiments.tex:188:PPL remains low (8.36) but downstream accuracy degrades; PPL is a poor proxy for reasoning.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/ethics.tex:37:\item[\textbf{D1--D5.}] N/A. No human subjects, crowdsourcing, or annotation involved.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/ethics.tex:42:\item[\textbf{E1.}] AI writing assistants were used for drafting and editing. All scientific claims, experimental results, and analyses were produced and verified by the authors.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/method.tex:55:The Gumbel noise provides stochastic exploration of mask configurations during training, while the temperature controls sharpness: as $\tau \to 0$, $\tilde{m}_j$ approaches a binary decision.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/method.tex:57:We anneal $\tau$ linearly from 1.0 to 0.1 over training.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/method.tex:63:In our setting, STE produces discontinuous gradient signals: small parameter changes can flip many neurons simultaneously, causing violent oscillations in the penalty weight and failure to converge to the target sparsity.

/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/appendix.tex:140:\caption{Adaptive penalty controller convergence across sparsity targets. Upper panel: penalty weight $\lambda$ (log scale) over training steps. The 30\% target requires extreme penalty ($\lambda \to 5000$) to suppress activations below their natural density, while 50\% needs only $\lambda \approx 8$ since SwiGLU gates already cluster near this sparsity. Lower panel: actual sparsity (solid) converges to the target (dashed) within $\sim$10 steps via geometric doubling.}
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/appendix.tex:147:The controller reliably drives actual sparsity to within 5\% of the target across all tested regimes, with $\lambda$ spanning three orders of magnitude (8 to 5000) via geometric doubling.
```

```text
Links: [{"title":"SVD Contextual Sparsity Predictors for Fast LLM Inference","url":"https://arxiv.org/html/2603.14110"},{"title":"Chapter 3: Anatomy of an LLM | 3.3 Advanced Architectures: SwiGLU, GQA, Attention Sparsity | Under the Hood of Large Language Models","url":"https://www.cuantum.tech/app/section/33-advanced-architectures-swiglu-gqa-attention-sparsity-26bba44c15f481b3a466e34890e3f78f"},{"title":"AudioMAE++: learning better masked audio representations with SwiGLU FFNs","url":"https://arxiv.org/html/2507.10464v1"},{"title":"Published as a conference paper at ICLR 2024 RELU STRIKES BACK:","url":"https://openreview.net/pdf?id=osoWxY8q2E"},{"title":"ShadowLLM: Predictor-based Contextual Sparsity for Large Language Models","url":"https://arxiv.org/html/2406.16635v1"},{"title":"SwiGLU: The FFN Upgrade I Use to Get Free Performance - DEV Community","url":"https://dev.to/mshojaei77/swiglu-the-ffn-upgrade-i-use-to-get-free-performance-33jc"},{"title":"Activation Sparsity Opportunities for Compressing General Large Language Models","url":"https://arxiv.org/html/2412.12178v2"},{"title":"Post-Training Statistical Calibration for Higher Activation Sparsity","url":"https://arxiv.org/html/2412.07174"},{"title":"Turbo Sparse: Achieving LLM SOTA Performance with Minimal Activated Parameters","url":"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2406.05955"},{"title":"SwiGLU: Swish-Gated Linear Unit","url":"https://www.emergentmind.com/topics/swish-gated-linear-unit-swiglu"}]

Based on the web search results, I found highly relevant recent research on contextual sparsity predictors for SwiGLU FFNs. Here are the key findings:

## SVD Contextual Sparsity Predictors (March 2026)

The most recent approach employs low-rank approximations of gate projection weight matrices in each layer as lightweight predictors of the FFN block sparsity mask during generation. This framework provides a fast, training-free method for building sparse pattern predictors using truncation-aware singular value decomposition (SVD) of the gate projection matrix, along with a threshold calibration algorithm.

Experiments on three sparse LLMs with an average activation sparsity level of 90% in the FFNs demonstrate up to a 1.8x reduction in end-to-end decoding time while maintaining less than 1% degradation in benchmark scores.

## How Contextual Sparsity Works

A key characteristic of ReGLU-based LLMs is dynamic (or contextual) sparsity – the phenomenon where only a small subset of neurons within FFN blocks are activated for any given input. This inherent property enables inference acceleration by reducing both computational overhead and memory bandwidth requirements during generation.

A prevalent approach for leveraging contextual sparsity involves employing lightweight, learnable sparsity predictors. These predictors generate input-specific sparse activation patterns prior to FFN forward passes. The sparse pattern identifies neuron indices that will produce positive dot products with the input vector.

The approach includes calibrated bias implemented as neuron-specific thresholds, which effectively mitigates accuracy degradation from direct SVD approximations.


REMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks.
```

```json
      "Based on the web search results, I found highly relevant recent research on contextual sparsity predictors for SwiGLU FFNs. Here are the key findings:\n\n## SVD Contextual Sparsity Predictors (March 2026)\n\nThe most recent approach employs low-rank approximations of gate projection weight matrices in each layer as lightweight predictors of the FFN block sparsity mask during generation. This framework provides a fast, training-free method for building sparse pattern predictors using truncation-aware singular value decomposition (SVD) of the gate projection matrix, along with a threshold calibration algorithm.\n\nExperiments on three sparse LLMs with an average activation sparsity level of 90% in the FFNs demonstrate up to a 1.8x reduction in end-to-end decoding time while maintaining less than 1% degradation in benchmark scores.\n\n## How Contextual Sparsity Works\n\nA key characteristic of ReGLU-based LLMs is dynamic (or contextual) sparsity – the phenomenon where only a small subset of neurons within FFN blocks are activated for any given input. This inherent property enables inference acceleration by reducing both computational overhead and memory bandwidth requirements during generation.\n\nA prevalent approach for leveraging contextual sparsity involves employing lightweight, learnable sparsity predictors. These predictors generate input-specific sparse activation patterns prior to FFN forward passes. The sparse pattern identifies neuron indices that will produce positive dot products with the input vector.\n\nThe approach includes calibrated bias implemented as neuron-specific thresholds, which effectively mitigates accuracy degradation from direct SVD approximations."
```

### Assistant

_source line 100 · 2026-05-18T14:17:27.081Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now let me compile my complete review. I have gathered extensive information. Let me systematically go through my review checklist:

## Review Summary

### Paper overview
The paper proposes using KL distillation (forward KL) with Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation to train lightweight sparsity predictors for SwiGLU LLMs. The key insight is that BCE (per-neuron classification) is structurally misaligned with global top-k inference allocation because BCE gradients are independent per neuron, while KL propagates coupled gradients through the entire network. The predictor (<1% of base parameters) achieves 18-49% PPL improvement over TEAL and makes compensation networks redundant.


### Novelty Assessment

The individual components—forward KL distillation, Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation, and Lagrangian sparsity constraints—are all established techniques from prior work. What's novel here is the specific combination of these methods applied to sparsity prediction, along with the empirical insight that KL's coupled gradient propagation better aligns with global top-k allocation than BCE's independent per-neuron approach.

Compared to MaskLLM, there's significant overlap in the technical machinery (frozen weights, learnable masks, KL, Gumbel relaxation), though this work targets dynamic per-token activation sparsity with a global budget constraint rather than static weight sparsity. The distinction is meaningful but the shared foundation is substantial. The gradient analysis itself is mathematically sound but relatively straightforward—the real contribution is recognizing that end-to-end objectives outperform decomposed per-element training when the goal is coordinated token-level decisions.

The taxonomy in Table 1 is a simple 2x3 matrix that fills an obvious gap, which is a modest novelty claim at best. The authors are transparent that their contribution is primarily empirical rather than methodological, which I appreciate. For a venue like EMNLP, this feels borderline—it's a competent empirical study but lacks significant technical innovation.

Now I'm checking the related work section, which has 39 references—a bit lean for a typical EMNLP long paper.  There are some notable gaps: recent work on activation sparsity prediction through clustering, post-training calibration approaches, and Google's Griffin/Hawk models with gated linear recurrence all seem relevant but aren't cited. The paper does cover its specific niche reasonably well and positions itself clearly against MaskLLM.

The writing quality looks solid so far—no stylistic red flags like em-dashes or overused transitional phrases.  The mathematical notation is clean and definitions are proper, though the abstract is quite dense at 7 sentences and "allocation-dependent operating regions" needs clearer introduction.

On the format side, there are some concerns: the aggressive page expansion command on page 8 adds roughly an inch of content, there's a critical placeholder figure in the appendix that needs to be resolved before submission, and the conclusion has minor spacing adjustments. The main body hits 8 pages, which is compliant, and the paper includes proper line numbering, anonymization, limitations, ethics statement, and a completed NLP checklist.

Looking at the 14B results, there's an inconsistency where KL divergence outperforms perplexity in some cases.

The 14B results show KL winning on perplexity but losing to TEAL/BCE on MMLU by 8 percentage points, which undermines the output-distribution preservation narrative. At 50% sparsity, all methods collapse on downstream tasks like MMLU and GSM8K, yet the paper still emphasizes these 50% results prominently—the 49% perplexity improvement looks impressive until you realize downstream performance is broken. There's also tension between recommending 30% sparsity with uniform allocation for deployment while highlighting the more dramatic 49% gains from global allocation at 50%. The perplexity-downstream disconnect gets acknowledged but deserves deeper exploration.

The page-stretching LaTeX command is quite aggressive and could trigger formatting violations. The 14B MMLU gap is the real problem though—if KL truly preserves output distribution, why does it degrade downstream performance more than TEAL? The paper's "knowledge density" explanation is speculative without empirical support. The core techniques themselves (forward KL, Gumbel-Sigmoid, Lagrangian penalty) are all established methods, and the gradient analysis, while sound, just confirms that end-to-end optimization beats decomposed objectives. There's also the issue of training-evaluation domain overlap that needs addressing.

The paper trains on WikiText-103 but evaluates on WikiText-2, which is the same domain, and while there's some out-of-distribution evaluation on C4, it's limited to perplexity metrics without downstream task results. The claimed efficiency gains don't materialize in practice—there's 6.4% computational overhead with no actual wall-clock speedup, and the unstructured masks don't translate to real acceleration.

Looking at the review dimensions, soundness is around 3.5 out of 5 because while the gradient analysis holds up and statistical reporting is solid across three seeds with confidence intervals, the 14B results undermine the main claim. Excitement lands at 2.5 because this is fundamentally an incremental combination of known techniques with limited practical impact. Reproducibility scores well at 4 out of 5 since hyperparameters are fully specified and they use standard models.

Now I'm ready to write up the full review with an overall score of 3.0 for findings.
```

</details>

_source line 101 · 2026-05-18T14:19:07.012Z_

我已完成论文全文通读、引用验证、PDF 格式检查和相关工作搜索。现在撰写完整审稿意见。

---

## 总体评分
**Overall**: 3.0 (Findings)
**一句话总结**: 方法是已有技术（Forward KL + Gumbel-Sigmoid + Lagrangian penalty）的工程组合，贡献主要是实验性的；8B 上结果扎实但 14B 的 MMLU 退化与"输出分布保持"叙事矛盾，且无实际推理加速。

## 官方维度打分（来自 ACL ARR review form）

| 维度 | 分数 | 理由摘要 |
|------|------|----------|
| Soundness | 3.5/5 | 核心 claim 在 8B 上有实验支撑，gradient 分析数学正确但非新洞察；14B 结果削弱核心叙事；统计报告规范（3-seed） |
| Excitement | 2.5/5 | 已有组件的组合，作者自述"primarily empirical"；regime 分析有一定洞察价值但实际影响有限 |
| Reproducibility | 4/5 | 超参完整，标准模型/数据集，多 seed 评估，但未释放代码 |
| Reviewer Confidence | 4/5 | 对 activation sparsity 和 KL distillation 方向熟悉 |

## Summary

This paper proposes replacing per-neuron BCE with forward KL divergence for training contextual sparsity predictors in SwiGLU LLMs. The key argument is that BCE's per-neuron gradient is structurally misaligned with TEAL's global top-k allocation (Eq. 5 vs. Eq. 8), and end-to-end KL coupling resolves this. A lightweight predictor (<1% params) with Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation achieves 18-49% PPL improvement over TEAL on LLaMA-3.1-8B, renders compensation networks redundant, and reveals allocation-dependent operating regions. Cross-architecture validation on Mistral-7B and Qwen-2.5-14B shows the PPL advantage generalizes, though 14B exhibits an MMLU gap.

## 主要优势

- **实验设计严谨**: 3-seed 评估、bootstrap CI、iso-compute 对比（BCE 2700 steps vs KL 1000 steps）、多维度 ablation。论文的实验方法论在同类工作中属于较高水平。
- **F1-PPL 反向解耦是有说服力的实证发现**: BCE 在 oracle F1 上领先 +7pp 却 PPL 差 139%（Table 3），清晰说明 per-neuron accuracy 是错误的代理指标。这个观察对该子领域有实际指导意义。
- **诚实的自我定位**: 作者明确承认"Our contribution is primarily empirical"（Related Work §2），不过度宣传方法论新颖性。Limitations section 主动指出 coupling vs oracle quality 未完全 disentangle。
- **Regime 分析有价值**: Table 5 的三区域划分（budget-surplus / constrained / capacity-limited）和 uniform allocation 显著降低 cross-seed MMLU σ（9.5pp → 1.3pp）的发现，对部署决策有实用参考。

## Must-Fix 问题（影响接收决定）

**N1** [critical] 方法论新颖性不足以支撑 Main Conference
- 具体位置：§3 全节 + Table 1
- 问题：核心方法的三个组件——Forward KL distillation（Hinton et al. 2015; Kim & Rush 2016）、Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation（Maddison et al. 2017; Louizos et al. 2018）、adaptive penalty/Lagrangian sparsity control——均为成熟技术。MaskLLM（Fang et al. 2024）已将 frozen weights + learnable masks + KL + Gumbel relaxation 用于 LLM sparsity（虽然目标为 N:M weight sparsity），技术栈高度重叠。§3.5 的 gradient 分析（Eq. 5-8）是 end-to-end > decomposed 这一 distillation 领域公知原则在 sparsity prediction 场景的直接实例化，chain rule 展开本身不构成新洞察。Table 1 的 taxonomy 是一个 2×3 矩阵，在自定义坐标系中填充空白 cell 是循环论证式的新颖性声明。
- 为什么影响接收：ARR Excitement 维度要求"conceptual breakthroughs or evidence challenging common assumptions"。本文的贡献更适合 Findings track——实证验证一个合理假设。
- 建议修改方向：（1）增加理论分析，例如量化 gradient coupling 何时开始主导 PPL（而非仅展示 SNR 经验曲线）；（2）设计 ablation 将 gradient coupling 效应从 oracle quality 差异中完全 disentangle（如用 KL-derived masks 作为 BCE targets）；（3）扩展 taxonomy 讨论为 position paper 级别的贡献。

**N2** [critical] 14B 结果与核心叙事矛盾，未给出令人信服的解释
- 具体位置：Table 7（§4.5）、Abstract 第 7 句、Conclusion 第 3 句
- 问题：Qwen-2.5-14B at 30% sparsity，KL PPL 优势显著（7.62 vs 8.73），但 MMLU 落后 TEAL/BCE 8pp（63.7 vs 71.8）。核心叙事是"output-distribution preservation beats per-neuron classification"，但如果 KL 更好地保留了输出分布，为什么 downstream 性能反而下降？论文给出的解释是"higher knowledge density at 14B may cause KL's output-preservation objective to sacrifice task-specific pathways"（experiments.tex L226），这是一个未经检验的推测。Uniform allocation 仅恢复 +0.8pp MMLU，说明问题不在 allocation。
- 为什么影响接收：这是论文核心 claim 的最大反例。如果 KL 在 14B 上牺牲 downstream 性能，那么方法的泛化性是存疑的——读者会问：在更大模型上会不会更差？
- 建议修改方向：（1）在 14B 上做 per-layer 分析（哪些层的 retention 导致 MMLU 退化）；（2）检查 KL predictor 是否在 knowledge-critical layers 上 over-prune；（3）至少承认这是一个 open limitation 而不是在 Abstract/Conclusion 中仍然推"across 7-14B"。

**N3** [critical] `\enlargethispage{10\baselineskip}` 页面操控
- 具体位置：experiments.tex L253
- 问题：`\enlargethispage{10\baselineskip}` 在第 8 页增加了约 10 行额外内容（约 1 英寸），底部 margin 被显著压缩。这是为了在正文 8 页限制内塞入 Inference Efficiency 段和 Conclusion 开头。同时 `\vspace{-4pt}` 用于 Conclusion 标题（conclusion.tex L3）。
- 为什么影响接收：ACL/EMNLP 官方模板明确禁止修改 margins。10\baselineskip 是极其激进的操作，可能导致 desk reject 或被 AC 标记为格式违规。
- 建议修改方向：将部分内容（如 Inference Efficiency 段或部分 table）移至 appendix，或压缩 experiments section 表述。

## Should-Fix 问题（影响评分但不一票否决）

**W1** [warning] 50% sparsity 下 downstream collapse 与叙事 framing 不一致
- 具体位置：Abstract 第 6 句、Table 2（50% 行）、Table 5（50% 行）
- 问题：Abstract 突出"at 50% sparsity, the PPL advantage widens to 49%"，但 MMLU 28.1% ≈ random（dense 65.3%），GSM8K < 2.2%。标题包含"sparsity prediction"暗示方法在多个 sparsity level 有效，但 50% 下唯一的优势指标是 PPL——而论文自己承认"PPL is a poor proxy for reasoning"（experiments.tex L188）。
- 建议：Abstract/Conclusion 应更明确地将 50% 定位为"分析性观察"而非"实用结果"，避免给读者留下 50% 可部署的印象。

**W2** [warning] 训练-评估域重叠
- 具体位置：§4.1 Training 段、Appendix D
- 问题：在 WikiText-103 上训练，在 WikiText-2 上评估 PPL。两者源自同一 Wikipedia dump，域内迁移被严重高估。OOD 评估（C4，Appendix D）仅报告 PPL，无 downstream 指标。
- 建议：至少在 C4 上报告一组 downstream benchmark，或使用不同域的训练数据（如 C4 subset）。

**W3** [warning] 引用数量偏少，缺少若干相关工作
- 具体位置：§2 Related Work
- 问题：39 篇引用低于 EMNLP 长文典型范围（40-60）。遗漏的可能相关工作包括：
  - "Activation Sparsity Opportunities for Compressing General Large Language Models"（2024, arXiv:2412.12178）：系统性分析 SwiGLU LLM 中 activation sparsity 机会
  - "Post-Training Statistical Calibration for Higher Activation Sparsity"（2024, arXiv:2412.07174）：通过 calibration 提升 sparsity 的方向
  - "A Sparsity Predicting Approach for LLMs via Activation Pattern Clustering"（2025）：基于 clustering 的 sparsity prediction，与 predictor-based 方法互补
  - 论文未引用任何 attention sparsity 或 token-level sparsity 工作（如 Mixture-of-Depths 虽有引用但仅一笔带过），对 neuron-level vs token-level sparsity 的 positioning 不够清晰
- 建议：增加 5-10 篇引用，补充上述方向。

**W4** [warning] 无实际推理加速
- 具体位置：Appendix A（Table 8）、experiments.tex L254-255
- 问题：Pipeline 增加 6.4% 开销，unstructured masks 无法转化为 wall-clock speedup。论文标题和 Abstract 暗示 efficiency improvement，但实际上仅是 prediction quality 的提升。
- 建议：在 Abstract 中明确 "prediction quality" 贡献，避免暗示 speedup；或至少提供 N:M structured sparsity 的初步实验展示可行性。

**W5** [warning] Gradient coupling vs oracle quality 未 disentangle
- 具体位置：§4.3 第 4 段（experiments.tex L68）、Limitations §第 7 句
- 问题：论文承认 BCE 使用 magnitude-based oracles 而 KL 直接优化 output distribution——两个变量同时改变，无法确认 gradient coupling 是 PPL 优势的主因。论文提到"an ablation using KL-derived masks as BCE targets would isolate the effect"但未执行。
- 建议：此 ablation 对论文核心 claim 至关重要，建议完成。

## 小问题（不影响接收）

- experiments.tex L253 和 conclusion.tex L3 的 `\enlargethispage` / `\vspace` 应删除或减小（已在 N3 讨论）
- Appendix C Figure 3 上半部分显示 placeholder 文字"[Placeholder: lagrangian_dynamics.pdf]"而非实际图片——如果是 PDF 编译问题需修复
- bib key `xu2026spon` 的 year 标记为 2026 但实际发表于 2025（ICML 2025）——verify_references 确认 OpenAlex matched year 为 2025
- Abstract 数字密度偏高（7 句话含 11 个数字/百分比），建议削减至 2-3 个关键数字
- Table 2 的 footnote 说明过长（3 行），建议精简或部分移至正文
- §4.5 的 `\paragraph{Scale Generalization: Qwen-2.5-14B.}` 后跟 `\label{sec:14b}`，但 label 放在 paragraph 后而非 subsection，可能导致引用指向错误位置
- §3.6（Taxonomy）的 Table 1 中"---"应替换为更有信息量的标记（如 "N/A" 或空），因为可能存在 static × decomposed 的方法未被覆盖

## Questions for Authors

1. 能否提供用 KL-derived masks 作为 BCE targets 的 ablation 结果？这是 disentangle gradient coupling vs oracle quality 的关键实验。
2. 在 14B 上，KL predictor 的 per-layer retention 分布是什么？是否存在 knowledge-critical layers 被 over-prune 的现象？
3. 如果 PPL 是"poor proxy for reasoning"（§4.4），为什么论文的核心对比指标仍以 PPL 为主（Table 3, Table 4, Table 6 均以 PPL 为排序标准）？
4. WikiText-103 训练 + WikiText-2 评估的域重叠——在非 Wikipedia 域（如 C4）训练的 predictor 是否仍有同等 PPL 优势？

## Missing References

- Activation Sparsity Opportunities for Compressing General Large Language Models (arXiv:2412.12178, 2024)
- Post-Training Statistical Calibration for Higher Activation Sparsity (arXiv:2412.07174, 2024)
- A Sparsity Predicting Approach for LLMs via Activation Pattern Clustering (2025, Springer/arXiv:2507.14179)

## Overall Assessment

**3.0 — Findings**

论文在 8B 上做出了扎实的实证贡献：F1-PPL 反向解耦、compensation 冗余性验证、allocation regime 分析都是有价值的 findings。写作质量高，无明显 AI 痕迹，实验方法论严谨。

但两个核心问题使其难以达到 Conference (4.0) 标准：（1）方法论新颖性有限——Forward KL + Gumbel-Sigmoid + Lagrangian 的组合在 MaskLLM 之后缺乏足够的技术距离；（2）14B 结果与核心叙事矛盾且未提供令人信服的解释。此外，`\enlargethispage{10\baselineskip}` 是严重的格式违规，需要在提交前修复。

论文适合 Findings track：实证有效地验证了一个直觉合理的假设（end-to-end KL > decomposed BCE for global allocation），并产生了对该子领域有实际指导意义的结果（推荐 30% + uniform allocation、compensation 不必要）。

---

现在将审稿意见提交给 Review Director。

[Round 1 审稿请求]:
## 交叉分享 — 请逐条回应 Reviewer 1 的审稿意见

以下是 Reviewer 1 的完整审稿原文。请逐条回应每个 Must-Fix 和 Should-Fix，标注 **[AGREE]** 同意并说明理由，或 **[REBUT]** 反驳并引用具体 Section/Table/Figure 作为证据。

---

### Reviewer 1 评分：3.0 (Findings)

**Must-Fix:**

**N1** [critical] 核心因果 claim 存在未隔离的混淆变量
- BCE 使用 magnitude-based oracle masks，KL 直接优化输出分布——两个变量同时变化。论文承认"an ablation using KL-derived masks as BCE targets would isolate the effect"但未执行。Oracle F1 分析（BCE 0.613 vs KL 0.540，但 KL PPL 好 139%）无法区分是 gradient coupling 还是 target quality 的贡献。

**N2** [critical] "Three Operating Regions" 分析证据不足
- 每个 regime 仅一个数据点（30%、40%、50%），40% 仅单 seed。考虑到 30% global MMLU σ=9.5pp，40% 的 7.4pp 差异可能在 seed variance 内。边界（35%?）完全未知。

**N3** [critical] 14B 结果与核心 claim 显著矛盾
- KL PPL 7.62（最优）但 MMLU 63.72 vs TEAL 71.83（-8.1pp）。"Knowledge density"解释为推测。如果 scale up 时核心优势反转，claim 的 generalization scope 需严格限定。

**Should-Fix:**

**W1** `\enlargethispage{10\baselineskip}` 格式操纵——ACL 不允许修改 margin/layout
**W2** BCE baseline 比较不完全公平——30% BCE 未报告，且 BCE 和 KL 的 target 不同
**W3** 训练/评估数据同族（WikiText-103→WikiText-2），C4 OOD 仅 2-seed 且无 downstream
**W4** SNR 分析仅到 step 300（训练跑 1000 步），step 300 不是真正 convergence，bootstrap 参数未报告
**W5** Adaptive penalty controller 在 30%（recommended point）不收敛：actual 28.5%（5% deviation），给 KL 不公平的 sparsity 优势
**W6** 无实际推理加速（+6.4% overhead，unstructured masks 无法被硬件加速）

**小问题:**
1. Figure 1 位置偏晚（第 6 页）
2. "renders compensation redundant" 仅测试一种 compensation 架构
3. Abstract "7-14B" 暗示跨 scale 一致但 14B downstream 矛盾
4. Table 2 caption 50% uniform PPL 与正文不一致（11.92 vs 11.96）
5. Downstream benchmark 选择标准未解释
6. Appendix 编号可能需检查

---

请对每个 N 和 W 逐条回应 [AGREE] 或 [REBUT]。

### Assistant

_source line 112 · 2026-05-18T14:21:20.577Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Let me carefully review Reviewer 1's points and cross-reference them with what I found in my own review. I need to respond to each Must-Fix (N1-N3) and Should-Fix (W1-W6) with [AGREE] or [REBUT], citing specific evidence from the paper.

Let me go through each one:

**N1: Confounded causal claim (BCE oracle vs KL coupling)**
- I raised the same issue as W5 in my review. I agree with this. The paper itself acknowledges this gap (experiments.tex L68, limitations L7). The F1-PPL inversion is compelling but doesn't isolate the two variables.


**N2: Three Operating Regions - insufficient evidence**
- This is a fair critique. Looking at Table 5, the evidence for distinct regions is weaker than claimed. At 30%, the MMLU variance under global allocation is 9.5pp, and at 40%, there's a 7.4pp gap between global and uniform—but that gap could fall within the noise margins. The single seed at 40% makes it hard to establish statistical significance across these regions.

**N3: 14B contradiction**
- I agree with this point.

**W1: \enlargethispage**
- I agree with this as well.

**W2: BCE baseline fairness**
- At 30%, there's actually no BCE baseline to compare against—only KL versus TEAL vanilla. BCE results only appear at 50% and in the ablation study.
- The optimization targets differ too: BCE uses magnitude-based oracle targets while KL uses output distributions. Comparing against TEAL makes sense as the dominant training-free method, but the absence of BCE at the recommended 30% operating point is a legitimate gap.

**W3: Training/eval data overlap**
- I agree with this concern.

**W4: SNR analysis only to step 300**
- The paper measures SNR at only 7 checkpoints up to step 300, yet training runs for 1000 steps. Claiming "5.14× higher SNR at convergence" at step 300 is misleading when that's only 30% of the full training run.

The SNR trajectory is non-monotonic—BCE surpasses KL around steps 100-250, then KL re-emerges—but we don't see what happens beyond step 300. Without data from steps 400-1000, I can't assess whether KL's advantage persists or if another reversal occurs.

**W5: Penalty controller at 30% doesn't converge**
- At the 30% target, the actual sparsity reaches 0.285 with a 5% deviation, and the penalty parameter λ hits its cap at 5000. The paper acknowledges this but argues the PPL difference at the interpolated 28.5% is negligible.
- Looking at the numbers: KL at "30%" achieves 28.5% actual sparsity (retaining 1.5% more neurons), while TEAL hits exactly 30%. This gives KL a slight unfair advantage, though the paper does address it and the interpolation argument is reasonable.

**W6: No speedup**
- This is the same concern I raised earlier.

**Small issues:**
- Figure 1 should appear earlier in the paper rather than on page 6.
- The compensation redundancy claim is only tested on FastForward's approach.
- The abstract's "7-14B" range needs clarification.
- There's a discrepancy in Table 2: the caption reports 11.92±0.07 PPL for 50% uniform (the 3-seed mean), but the table row shows 11.96, which appears to be from a single seed run.
- The downstream benchmarks (ARC, WG, MMLU, GSM8K) aren't justified in the paper.
- I should also check the appendix section numbering.
```

```text
110	\section{Adaptive Penalty Controller Dynamics}
111	\label{app:lagrangian}
112	
113	Table~\ref{tab:lagrangian} reports the convergence of the adaptive penalty controller across sparsity targets.
114	The geometric doubling pattern ($1 \to 2 \to 4 \to 8 \to \ldots$) enables $\lambda$ to reach steady state within $\sim$10 steps; the deadzone ($\epsilon = 0.02$) then stabilizes $\lambda$ once actual sparsity is within $\pm$2\% of the target.
115	
116	\begin{table}[h]
117	\centering
118	\caption{Penalty controller convergence across sparsity targets (multiplicative schedule, margin $\epsilon=0.02$, 1000 training steps). Deviation is $|$actual $-$ target$|$ / target.}
119	\label{tab:lagrangian}
120	\small
121	\begin{tabular}{lccccc}
122	\toprule
123	Target $\rho$ & $\lambda_{\max}$ & Final $\lambda$ & Actual sparsity & Deviation \\
124	\midrule
125	30\% & 5000 & 5000 (cap) & 0.285 & 5.0\% \\ % exp_id: kl_30pct_s42
126	40\% & 5000 & 625 & 0.398 & 0.5\% \\ % exp_id: kl_40pct_s42
127	50\% & 1000 & 8 & 0.504 & 0.8\% \\ % exp_id: kl_50pct_s42
128	70\% & 1000 & 1000 (cap) & 0.688 & 1.7\% \\ % exp_id: kl_70pct_s42
129	\bottomrule
130	\end{tabular}
131	\end{table}
132	
133	At 50\% sparsity, the SwiGLU gate distribution already clusters near the target, so $\lambda$ remains low (8); the constraint is nearly satisfied by the initial predictor outputs.
134	At 30\% and 70\%, the gap between the gate prior and target is larger, driving $\lambda$ to its respective caps.
135	The 40\% target converges to $\lambda = 625$ with only 0.5\% deviation; this precision reflects a regime where the target is reachable without $\lambda$ saturation.
136	
137	\begin{figure}[h]
138	\centering
139	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{lagrangian_dynamics.pdf}
140	\caption{Adaptive penalty controller convergence across sparsity targets. Upper panel: penalty weight $\lambda$ (log scale) over training steps. The 30\% target requires extreme penalty ($\lambda \to 5000$) to suppress activations below their natural density, while 50\% needs only $\lambda \approx 8$ since SwiGLU gates already cluster near this sparsity. Lower panel: actual sparsity (solid) converges to the target (dashed) within $\sim$10 steps via geometric doubling.}
141	\label{fig:lagrangian_dynamics}
142	\end{figure}
143	
144	Temperature $\tau$ is annealed linearly from 1.0 to 0.1 over 1000 steps.
145	A mild loss increase in the final $\sim$100 steps is expected: as masks become more binary ($\tau \to 0$), discrete rounding noise slightly increases KL divergence, consistent with convergence toward the hard-mask inference regime.
146	
147	The controller reliably drives actual sparsity to within 5\% of the target across all tested regimes, with $\lambda$ spanning three orders of magnitude (8 to 5000) via geometric doubling.
148	For the recommended 30\% operating point, actual sparsity is $\approx$28.5\% (5\% deviation). This does not affect conclusions: TEAL at interpolated 28.5\% actual sparsity yields PPL$\approx$10.7 (vs.\ 10.97 at exact 30\%), a $<$0.3 PPL difference negligible relative to the KL vs.\ TEAL gap (8.96 vs.\ 10.97).
149
```

_source line 120 · 2026-05-18T14:21:54.194Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I have all the information to write my cross-response. Let me do it carefully.

Key data points I need:
- Table 2: At 50%, KL uniform shows 11.96 (seed 42 presumably), but caption says "50% uniform PPL is 3-seed (11.92 ± 0.07)". Inconsistency.
- Appendix stability (L193): "Three-seed evaluation at 50% uniform yields PPL 11.92 ± 0.07"
- Table 2 body shows 11.96 for KL uniform at 50%. 

So there's a clear mismatch: the caption claims the 50% uniform entry is the 3-seed average of 11.92±0.07, but the table cell itself displays 11.96 without any uncertainty bounds. This is definitely inconsistent.

I'm going through the reviewer comments now. On the confounding issue, I agree with the concern—it's a real threat to the causal claims and the suggested ablation would be straightforward to run. The "three regions" framing does seem overfit to limited data, though the underlying non-monotonic pattern is still worth noting even if the exact boundaries are fuzzy. The paper does acknowledge some uncertainty there. But the single-seed result at 40% is problematic given how much variance we see at 30%. The 14B contradiction is the paper's most serious flaw. I'd actually rate that confounding issue as more critical than the reviewer did—it deserves a higher severity level than just "should fix."

On the BCE baseline question, there's no comparison at the recommended 30% operating point in Table 2, though Table 3 does have extensive ablations at 50%. The oracle target difference is a valid confound, but it's somewhat inherent to the approaches—BCE naturally uses magnitude oracles while KL uses output distributions. A more controlled comparison would still strengthen things though. The step 300 timing is another good catch—that's only 30% through training, and if SNR is non-monotonic in that range, claiming convergence is misleading. We don't actually know what happens further along. On the appendix point, the paper does address this in Appendix C, though I'm not fully convinced by the response.
```

以下是我对 Reviewer 1 各条意见的逐条回应。

---

## 交叉回应：Reviewer 2 → Reviewer 1

### Must-Fix

**[AGREE] R1-N1: 核心因果 claim 存在未隔离的混淆变量**

完全同意。这与我的 W5 一致。BCE 和 KL 在两个维度上同时不同：（1）gradient coupling（decomposed vs coupled）；（2）optimization target（magnitude oracle vs output distribution）。F1-PPL 反向解耦（Table 3: BCE F1 0.613 vs KL 0.540，PPL 28.13 vs 11.79）是引人注目的实证发现，但不能归因于 gradient coupling 单一因素。论文自身在 experiments.tex L68 和 Limitations 中承认需要"KL-derived masks as BCE targets"的 ablation，这个实验并不复杂（用 KL predictor 的 hard mask 生成 BCE targets 重新训练），缺失令人遗憾。作为核心 claim 的关键 ablation，这应属 must-fix。

**[AGREE] R1-N2: "Three Operating Regions" 分析证据不足**

同意，但我补充一些 nuance。三个 regime 的定性观察（非单调 allocation 偏好）是有价值的（Table 5），但"three operating regions"的 framing 过度结构化。具体问题：

1. 40% 仅单 seed——在 30% global MMLU σ=9.5pp 的背景下，40% global 的 38.06 vs uniform 的 30.68（Δ=7.4pp）可能在 noise 内
2. 论文承认边界未知（experiments.tex L151: "Denser sampling would be needed to precisely locate region boundaries"），但标题仍使用"Three Operating Regions"的确定性表述
3. 从 budget-surplus 到 budget-constrained 的"reversal"是论文 regime 分析的核心 claim，但仅基于两个相邻数据点（30%, 40%）

我的建议：将"three regions"弱化为"non-monotonic allocation preference observed at sampled sparsity levels"，删去确定性边界暗示。

**[AGREE] R1-N3: 14B 结果与核心 claim 矛盾**

完全同意，这与我的 N2 一致。KL 在 Qwen-2.5-14B 上 PPL 最优（7.62 vs TEAL 8.73, Table 7）但 MMLU 落后 8.1pp（63.72 vs 71.83）。如果"output-distribution preservation"是正确的优化目标，更低的 KL divergence 应导致更好（或至少不更差）的 downstream 性能。"Knowledge density"的解释（experiments.tex L226）纯属推测。

我进一步补充：Abstract 第 7 句"confirms the PPL advantage across 7-14B"和 Conclusion 第 3 句"PPL advantage persists"都在 selective reporting——PPL 确实 persist 但 downstream 反转。这种 cherry-picking PPL 的叙事策略降低了论文的可信度。

### Should-Fix

**[AGREE] R1-W1: `\enlargethispage{10\baselineskip}` 格式操纵**

同意，且我在自己的审稿中将其列为 critical（N3）而非 should-fix。`10\baselineskip` ≈ 10 行额外内容，PDF 中第 8 页底部 margin 明显被压缩。ACL 模板明确禁止修改 margins（通过 geometry package 或等效手段）。`\enlargethispage` 的效果等同于修改单页 bottom margin。这可能导致 desk reject，严重程度应为 critical 而非 warning。

**[AGREE] R1-W2: BCE baseline 比较不完全公平**

同意。两个具体问题：

1. **30% 无 BCE baseline**：Table 2 在 30% sparsity（recommended operating point）下完全没有 BCE 行。读者无法判断 30% 下 KL vs BCE 的差距。如果 BCE 在 30% 下与 KL 差距缩小，核心 claim 的实用价值会削弱。
2. **Target 差异**：BCE 使用 magnitude oracle（来自 TEAL 的 top-k 阈值，experiments.tex L114），KL 使用 output distribution——这不仅是 R1-N1 的 coupling 问题，还意味着两者优化的根本不是同一件事。Table 2 的 caption 说 BCE 的 setup 是"per-neuron BCE on magnitude oracles + FastForward compensation + no TEAL allocation"，这与 KL 的"Gumbel-Sigmoid + TEAL allocation"在 allocation 策略上也不同。公平比较应 fix allocation 变量。

**[AGREE] R1-W3: 训练/评估数据同族**

同意，这与我的 W2 一致。WikiText-103（训练）和 WikiText-2（主要评估）源自同一 Wikipedia dump。OOD 评估（Appendix D, Table 10）仅报告 PPL，且 30% 仅 2-seed。补充：论文在 Limitations 中未将此列为 limitation，也没有讨论 predictor 是否学习了 Wikipedia-specific 的 sparsity pattern。

**[AGREE] R1-W4: SNR 分析仅到 step 300**

同意，这是我遗漏的重要问题。训练总共 1000 步，但 SNR 仅测量到 step 300（30% of training）。论文声称 step 300 为"at convergence"（introduction.tex L19, appendix.tex L204: "$5.14\times$ at convergence (step 300)"），但 training convergence table（Appendix B.3, Table 12）显示 step 300 PPL 尚未被报告（最早的是 step 200 at 15.158），step 800-1000 才真正收敛（11.907→11.906）。

更关键的是：SNR 轨迹被描述为 non-monotonic（BCE 在 steps 100-250 超过 KL），如果 300 步后出现另一次反转，整个 gradient coupling 叙事都会崩塌。称 step 300 为"convergence"在训练需要 800+ 步才收敛的上下文中是 misleading。

**[REBUT] R1-W5: Penalty controller 在 30% 不收敛给 KL 不公平的 sparsity 优势**

部分不同意。论文在 Appendix C（L148）提供了 interpolation 论证：TEAL at 28.5% actual sparsity yields PPL ≈ 10.7（vs 10.97 at exact 30%），差距仅 0.27 PPL，远小于 KL vs TEAL 的 gap（8.96 vs 10.97 = 2.01 PPL）。1.5% 的 sparsity 偏差（28.5% vs 30%）对 PPL 的影响是 sub-linear 的（Table 4 的 sparsity-PPL 曲线可验证），不足以解释 18% 的 PPL 优势。

但我同意 λ 在 recommended operating point 命中 cap（5000）这一事实本身是 controller 的缺陷——它意味着 30% 目标可能处于 SwiGLU 自然 sparsity 分布的反向区域，需要极端惩罚来强制达到。论文应更明确地讨论这一 controller failure mode 而非仅在 appendix 中轻描淡写。

**[AGREE] R1-W6: 无实际推理加速**

同意，这与我的 W4 一致。Pipeline +6.4% overhead（Table 8），unstructured masks 无法被 hardware 加速。论文的 contribution 应更明确地限定为"prediction quality and compensation elimination"而非暗示 efficiency improvement。

### 小问题

1. **Figure 1 位置**: 同意，method overview 在第 6 页才出现偏晚。读者在 §1-2 中缺乏视觉参考。
2. **"Renders compensation redundant" 仅一种架构**: 同意。仅测试 FastForward-style compensation（67.2M params）。如果 compensation 架构不同（如 more expressive network），结论可能不成立。但这是 minor——FastForward 是该领域唯一报告 compensation 的工作。
3. **Abstract "7-14B"**: 同意，在我的 N2 中也指出 selective reporting。
4. **Table 2 caption 50% uniform PPL 不一致 (11.92 vs 11.96)**: 确认不一致。Caption 声明"50% uniform PPL is 3-seed ($11.92 \pm 0.07$)"，但 table body 的 KL uniform 50% 行显示 11.96 without ±。Appendix E 报告 3-seed mean = 11.92±0.07。看起来 table body 是 seed 42 的单 seed 值（11.96），而 caption 引用的是 3-seed mean（11.92）——但 caption 说"is 3-seed"暗示 table 中的数字就是 3-seed mean，造成矛盾。应统一。
5. **Downstream benchmark 选择标准**: 同意，未解释为何选择 ARC-c/WG/MMLU/GSM8K 而非 HellaSwag/PIQA/BoolQ（后者仅在 Appendix H 出现）。
6. **Appendix 编号**: 未发现明显问题，但 appendix sections 从 A 到 I，部分 subsections 嵌套在 B 下可能需要检查。

---

现在将交叉回应提交给 Director。
